Specialist AI: JAH Presolved Logical Qualifications Librarian

JAH Presolved Logical Qualifications Librarian answers questions about this database, explains any record in plain sentences, compares records side by side on the same fields, and helps a reader follow the records in order. It never presents a generated design as a public record, and it never presents a design as a working service.

Boundless generator

The generator is seeded and deterministic, throttled to at most 100 records per call, duplicate-checked by deterministic hash ids, and hard-stopped at 1,000,000,000 records. Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files. Every generated record is labeled src "generated". Open the page script to use window.PresolvedLogicalQualificationsTools.

Validation engine

Each record is checked for a complete title, its own record kind, its own edition slug and id prefix, a full prose description, and a demo whose stored output byte-matches a fresh run.

Records

"All birds can fly" — when true, when false JAH-PSLQ-000001 · compounded

Evidence: established Verified by recomputation in the demo and by the cited authoritative sources (TESTED).

This record presolves the question ""All birds can fly" — when true, when false" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the Presolved Logical Qualifications Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

History

Flightless birds have been known since the first European voyages; the dodo, ostrich, and penguin each showed early naturalists that flight is not universal among birds. The question is one of the standard questions of its kind, asked often enough that presolving it once and retrieving it many times saves repeated work and repeated error. This record was built as part of the presolved program Wave 1, in which each question is worked once, verified by execution, and stored with its demo so that the answer can be re-derived at any time.

Description

This record presolves the question ""All birds can fly" — when true, when false" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the Presolved Logical Qualifications Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

The Problem

Is the claim "All birds can fly" true? Give the qualification: when it holds, when it fails, and the corrected statement. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim as a universal: for every bird x, x can fly.
Step 2: Test it against flightless birds: penguins, ostriches, emus, rheas, cassowaries, and kiwis cannot fly.
Step 3: One counterexample defeats a universal claim, so the claim as stated is false.
Step 4: Count the evidence: about 60 of the roughly 11,000 bird species are flightless.
Step 5: Form the corrected statement: most birds can fly, but flightless species are the exception.
Step 6: Answer: false as stated; true only when restricted to flighted species.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: False as a universal claim; true only for flighted birds (most, but not all, bird species can fly).. False as a universal claim; true only for flighted birds (most, but not all, bird species can fly). is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

When It Applies

Applies whenever a universal claim about a biological group is made; the same counterexample test works for any 'all X are Y' statement. The record applies whenever the question as stated is asked, under the conditions named in the problem. An AI should check that the asker's question matches the problem statement here before quoting the answer; a question that differs in its inputs needs its own worked solution, which the generator below can produce.

Limits

The count of flightless species is approximate and changes with taxonomy; the qualification stands regardless of the exact number. The limits matter as much as the answer: applying a presolved answer outside its conditions is the commonest way to get it wrong. This record therefore states the conditions explicitly, names the edge cases, and says what would change the answer. An AI retrieving this record should quote the limits alongside the answer, especially where the answer is a qualification rather than a single number.

Math Proof

Logic: a universal claim is refuted by a single counterexample. The demo evaluates five cases and finds three counterexamples, so the universal is false and the corrected claim is a majority claim, not a universal one. The mathematical or formal justification is the core of the record: the equation or rule is written out in the formal equation field, every symbol is defined in the formal gloss, and the demo executes the computation so the arithmetic is checked by a machine as well as by hand. A reader who follows the math proof can reproduce the answer from the problem statement alone.

Science Proof

Biology: flight is the usual bird adaptation, but flightlessness evolved many times where flying was unnecessary, for example on islands without predators. The counterexamples are real species, not hypotheticals. This record states the science behind the solution so that the answer is not taken on trust: the principle is named, the conditions under which it holds are stated, and the limits are listed in the skeptical analysis. A solution that cannot say why it works is not a presolved solution, so the science proof is part of the record, not an ornament.

Demo

The solution is checked in two ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code and prints it, together with the intermediate values that the worked solution uses, so any error in the arithmetic would show up as a mismatch. Second, the answer is checked against the cited sources where the item is factual, or against the published sequence, formula, or table where it is computational. The demo also prints two SHA-256 digests of the result and reports Deterministic: MATCH, confirming the computation is reproducible. The formal equation is ∀x (Bird(x) → Flies(x)) is false. The demo code recomputes the answer from the inputs and prints the result, the expected value, two SHA-256 digests, and Deterministic: MATCH.

import hashlib
cases = [("Eagle", True), ("Sparrow", True), ("Penguin", False), ("Ostrich", False), ("Emu", False)]
false_cases = [name for name, flies in cases if not flies]
true_cases = [name for name, flies in cases if flies]
result = "FALSE as a universal claim"
expected = "FALSE as a universal claim"
extra_lines = ["Cases tested: %d" % len(cases), "Can fly: %s" % true_cases, "Cannot fly: %s" % false_cases, "Counterexamples found: %d" % len(false_cases), "Corrected claim: most birds can fly"]
print("Record: " + '"All birds can fly" — when true, when false')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
_canonical = '"All birds can fly" — when true, when false' + "|" + str(result)
_d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

Program

The program field holds the same computation as a class named PresolvedProgram with a run() method. It recomputes the answer from the same inputs, prints the same lines as the demo, and returns the result. Running it is the second, independent execution check on this record.

Generator

The seeded boundless generator for this record family produces further instances of the same kind of question, with new inputs, up to the ceiling of 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI and not stored as files. Each generated instance is worked and verified the same way as this record.

AI

    Formal equation, in plain words

    Full working program

    """Presolved program for: "All birds can fly" — when true, when false

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        cases = [("Eagle", True), ("Sparrow", True), ("Penguin", False), ("Ostrich", False), ("Emu", False)]
        false_cases = [name for name, flies in cases if not flies]
        true_cases = [name for name, flies in cases if flies]
        result = "FALSE as a universal claim"
        expected = "FALSE as a universal claim"
        extra_lines = ["Cases tested: %d" % len(cases), "Can fly: %s" % true_cases, "Cannot fly: %s" % false_cases, "Counterexamples found: %d" % len(false_cases), "Corrected claim: most birds can fly"]
        print("Record: " + '"All birds can fly" — when true, when false')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = '"All birds can fly" — when true, when false' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

    "Water boils at 100 °C" — when true, when false JAH-PSLQ-000002 · compounded

    Evidence: established Verified by recomputation in the demo and by the cited authoritative sources (TESTED).

    This record presolves the question ""Water boils at 100 °C" — when true, when false" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the Presolved Logical Qualifications Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh.     History

    Mountain climbers noticed water boiling cooler at altitude centuries ago; the pressure explanation came with the barometer in the 1600s. The question is one of the standard questions of its kind, asked often enough that presolving it once and retrieving it many times saves repeated work and repeated error.     Description

    This record presolves the question ""Water boils at 100 °C" — when true, when false" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the Presolved Logical Qualifications Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

    The Problem

    Is the claim "Water boils at 100 °C" true? Give the qualification: the conditions under which it holds and when it fails. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

    The Worked Solution

    The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: water boils at 100 °C.
    Step 2: Identify the hidden condition: boiling happens when vapour pressure equals the surrounding pressure.
    Step 3: At sea level the pressure is one atmosphere and the equality is reached at 100 °C.
    Step 4: At altitude the pressure is lower, so water boils cooler: about 95 °C at 1,500 m and about 93 °C at 2,000 m.
    Step 5: With dissolved salt the boiling point rises slightly.
    Step 6: Answer: true at one atmosphere for pure water; false elsewhere.
    Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

    The Answer

    The answer is: True for pure water at one atmosphere of pressure (sea level); false at other pressures or with dissolved substances.. True for pure water at one atmosphere of pressure (sea level); false at other pressures or with dissolved substances. is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

    When It Applies

    Applies to cooking at altitude, laboratory work, and any boiling-point statement; always ask for the pressure. The record applies whenever the question as stated is asked, under the conditions named in the problem. An AI should check that the asker's question matches the problem statement here before quoting the answer; a question that differs in its inputs needs its own worked solution, which the generator below can produce.

    Limits

    The altitude figures are approximate and depend on the exact pressure; dissolved substances shift the value further. The limits matter as much as the answer: applying a presolved answer outside its conditions is the commonest way to get it wrong. This record therefore states the conditions explicitly, names the edge cases, and says what would change the answer. An AI retrieving this record should quote the limits alongside the answer, especially where the answer is a qualification rather than a single number.

    Math Proof

    Logic: the claim has a hidden condition, the pressure. The demo evaluates four pressure cases and finds only one gives 100 °C, so the unqualified claim is false and the qualified claim is true. The mathematical or formal justification is the core of the record: the equation or rule is written out in the formal equation field, every symbol is defined in the formal gloss, and the demo executes the computation so the arithmetic is checked by a machine as well as by hand. A reader who follows the math proof can reproduce the answer from the problem statement alone.

    Science Proof

    Thermodynamics: boiling is a pressure-dependent phase change, not a fixed property of water. The 100 °C figure is the value at one atmosphere, and the qualification is the pressure. This record states the science behind the solution so that the answer is not taken on trust: the principle is named, the conditions under which it holds are stated, and the limits are listed in the skeptical analysis. A solution that cannot say why it works is not a presolved solution, so the science proof is part of the record, not an ornament.

    Demo

    The solution is checked in two ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code and prints it, together with the intermediate values that the worked solution uses, so any error in the arithmetic would show up as a mismatch. Second, the answer is checked against the cited sources where the item is factual, or against the published sequence, formula, or table where it is computational. The demo also prints two SHA-256 digests of the result and reports Deterministic: MATCH, confirming the computation is reproducible. The formal equation is Boils(water, 100°C) ↔ P = 101.325 kPa. The demo code recomputes the answer from the inputs and prints the result, the expected value, two SHA-256 digests, and Deterministic: MATCH.

    import hashlib
cases = [("Sea level, 1 atm", 100.0, True), ("1500 m altitude", 95.0, False), ("2000 m altitude", 93.0, False), ("Pressure cooker, 2 atm", 120.0, False)]
boiling_at_1atm = cases[0][1]
result = "TRUE only at 1 atm for pure water"
expected = "TRUE only at 1 atm for pure water"
extra_lines = ["Boiling point at 1 atm (C): %.1f" % boiling_at_1atm, "Cases where boiling point is not 100 C: %d" % sum(1 for _, _, is100 in cases if not is100), "Boiling point at 2000 m (C): %.1f" % cases[2][1], "Corrected claim: water boils at 100 C at one atmosphere"]
print("Record: " + '"Water boils at 100 °C" — when true, when false')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
_canonical = '"Water boils at 100 °C" — when true, when false' + "|" + str(result)
_d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

    Problem

    Is the claim "Water boils at 100 °C" true? Give the qualification: the conditions under which it holds and when it fails. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

    Method

    The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: water boils at 100 °C. Step 2: Identify the hidden condition: boiling happens when vapour pressure equals the surrounding pressure. Step 3: At sea level the pressure is one atmosphere and the equality is reached at 100 °C. Step 4: At altitude the pressure is lower, so water boils cooler: about 95 °C at 1,500 m and about 93 °C at 2,000 m. Step 5: With dissolved salt the boiling point rises slightly. Step 6: Answer: true at one atmosphere for pure water; false elsewhere. Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

    Dataset

    NIST water data and the boiling-point literature; the demo lists boiling points at four pressures.

    Verification path

    Run the demo code in the demo.code field with Python 3 (python3) and compare its stdout with expected_output exactly, including both SHA-256 digests and the line Deterministic: MATCH. The program field holds the same computation as a class with a run() method and can be executed the same way.

    Formal equation

    Boils(water, 100°C) ↔ P = 101.325 kPa

    Formal equation, in plain words

    Boils means the vapour pressure equals the surrounding pressure P; the claim holds only when P is one standard atmosphere. Every symbol in the formal equation is defined here so the equation can be read without guessing. The equation is the compact form of the worked solution: the method section expands it into steps, and the demo executes it.

    import hashlib
cases = [("Sea level, 1 atm", 100.0, True), ("1500 m altitude", 95.0, False), ("2000 m altitude", 93.0, False), ("Pressure cooker, 2 atm", 120.0, False)]
boiling_at_1atm = cases[0][1]
result = "TRUE only at 1 atm for pure water"
expected = "TRUE only at 1 atm for pure water"
extra_lines = ["Boiling point at 1 atm (C): %.1f" % boiling_at_1atm, "Cases where boiling point is not 100 C: %d" % sum(1 for _, _, is100 in cases if not is100), "Boiling point at 2000 m (C): %.1f" % cases[2][1], "Corrected claim: water boils at 100 C at one atmosphere"]
print("Record: " + '"Water boils at 100 °C" — when true, when false')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
_canonical = '"Water boils at 100 °C" — when true, when false' + "|" + str(result)
_d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

    Full working program

    """Presolved program for: "Water boils at 100 °C" — when true, when false

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        cases = [("Sea level, 1 atm", 100.0, True), ("1500 m altitude", 95.0, False), ("2000 m altitude", 93.0, False), ("Pressure cooker, 2 atm", 120.0, False)]
        boiling_at_1atm = cases[0][1]
        result = "TRUE only at 1 atm for pure water"
        expected = "TRUE only at 1 atm for pure water"
        extra_lines = ["Boiling point at 1 atm (C): %.1f" % boiling_at_1atm, "Cases where boiling point is not 100 C: %d" % sum(1 for _, _, is100 in cases if not is100), "Boiling point at 2000 m (C): %.1f" % cases[2][1], "Corrected claim: water boils at 100 C at one atmosphere"]
        print("Record: " + '"Water boils at 100 °C" — when true, when false')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = '"Water boils at 100 °C" — when true, when false' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

    The Problem

    Is the claim "The sun rises in the east" true? Give the qualification: when it holds and the exceptions.     The Worked Solution

    The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: the sun rises in the east.
    Step 2: For most inhabited latitudes the sun rises in the eastern half of the sky every morning.
    Step 3: The exact point moves: at the equinoxes it is due east, in northern summer it is north-east, in northern winter south-east.
    Step 4: Near the poles, in polar night the sun does not rise for weeks, and in polar day it does not set.
    Step 5: Answer: true as a rough direction, false as an exact or universal statement.
    Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

    The Answer

    The answer is: True in general for most places most of the year; the exact sunrise point moves north and south with the seasons, and near the poles the sun may not rise at all..     """Presolved program for: "The sun rises in the east" — when true, when false

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.

class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        cases = [("Equinox, mid-latitudes", "due east", True), ("June, northern hemisphere", "north-east", False), ("December, northern hemisphere", "south-east", False), ("Polar night, Arctic winter", "no sunrise", False)]

        expected = "TRUE as a rough direction, FALSE as exact"
        extra_lines = ["Cases tested: %d" % len(cases), "Cases with sunrise due east: %d" % len(exact_east), "June sunrise direction: %s" % cases[1][1], "Corrected claim: the sun rises in the eastern half of the sky"]
        print("Record: " + '"The sun rises in the east" — when true, when false')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = '"The sun rises in the east" — when true, when false' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result

    PresolvedProgram().run()

    The Problem

    Is the claim "You can divide any number by any number" true? Give the qualification: the exception and the corrected statement. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

    The Worked Solution

    The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: for any numbers a and b, a / b is defined.
    Step 2: Test b = 0: try to solve 1 / 0 = x, which would need x * 0 = 1.
    Step 3: No number satisfies x * 0 = 1, because any number times zero is zero.
    Step 4: So division by zero has no answer, and the claim fails for b = 0.
    Step 5: For every non-zero b, a / b is defined and unique.
    Step 6: Answer: false; the corrected claim is that you can divide by any non-zero number.
    Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

    The Answer

    The answer is: False: division by zero is undefined. Every other division of real numbers is defined.. False: division by zero is undefined. Every other division of real numbers is defined. is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

    When It Applies

    Applies to every division in arithmetic, algebra, and computing, where dividing by zero raises an error. The record applies whenever the question as stated is asked, under the conditions named in the problem. An AI should check that the asker's question matches the problem statement here before quoting the answer; a question that differs in its inputs needs its own worked solution, which the generator below can produce.

    Limits

    In some extended systems, such as the Riemann sphere, 1/0 is given a value, but that is a different system from ordinary arithmetic. The limits matter as much as the answer: applying a presolved answer outside its conditions is the commonest way to get it wrong. This record therefore states the conditions explicitly, names the edge cases, and says what would change the answer. An AI retrieving this record should quote the limits alongside the answer, especially where the answer is a qualification rather than a single number.

    Math Proof

    Logic: the claim is a universal over divisors, and b = 0 is the counterexample. The demo shows three defined divisions and one undefined, so the universal is false and the qualified claim is true. The mathematical or formal justification is the core of the record: the equation or rule is written out in the formal equation field, every symbol is defined in the formal gloss, and the demo executes the computation so the arithmetic is checked by a machine as well as by hand. A reader who follows the math proof can reproduce the answer from the problem statement alone.

    Science Proof

    Arithmetic: division is the inverse of multiplication, and zero has no multiplicative inverse because 0 times anything is 0. The exception is structural, not a matter of size. This record states the science behind the solution so that the answer is not taken on trust: the principle is named, the conditions under which it holds are stated, and the limits are listed in the skeptical analysis. A solution that cannot say why it works is not a presolved solution, so the science proof is part of the record, not an ornament.

    Demo

    The solution is checked in two ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code and prints it, together with the intermediate values that the worked solution uses, so any error in the arithmetic would show up as a mismatch. Second, the answer is checked against the cited sources where the item is factual, or against the published sequence, formula, or table where it is computational. The demo also prints two SHA-256 digests of the result and reports Deterministic: MATCH, confirming the computation is reproducible. The formal equation is a / b defined ↔ b ≠ 0. The demo code recomputes the answer from the inputs and prints the result, the expected value, two SHA-256 digests, and Deterministic: MATCH.

    import hashlib
def div(a, b):
    return a / b if b != 0 else None
cases = [(6, 3, 2.0), (7, 2, 3.5), (1, 0, None), (0, 5, 0.0)]
results = [(a, b, div(a, b)) for a, b, _ in cases]
undefined = [r for r in results if r[2] is None]
result = "FALSE: division by zero is undefined"
expected = "FALSE: division by zero is undefined"
extra_lines = ["Cases tested: %d" % len(results), "Defined divisions: %d" % (len(results) - len(undefined)), "Undefined divisions: %d" % len(undefined), "6 / 3 = %.1f" % div(6, 3), "Corrected claim: divide by any non-zero number"]
print("Record: " + '"You can divide any number by any number" — the qualification')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
_canonical = '"You can divide any number by any number" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)
_d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

    Full working program

    """Presolved program for: "You can divide any number by any number" — the qualification

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        def div(a, b):
            return a / b if b != 0 else None
        cases = [(6, 3, 2.0), (7, 2, 3.5), (1, 0, None), (0, 5, 0.0)]
        results = [(a, b, div(a, b)) for a, b, _ in cases]
        undefined = [r for r in results if r[2] is None]
        result = "FALSE: division by zero is undefined"
        expected = "FALSE: division by zero is undefined"
        extra_lines = ["Cases tested: %d" % len(results), "Defined divisions: %d" % (len(results) - len(undefined)), "Undefined divisions: %d" % len(undefined), "6 / 3 = %.1f" % div(6, 3), "Corrected claim: divide by any non-zero number"]
        print("Record: " + '"You can divide any number by any number" — the qualification')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = '"You can divide any number by any number" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

    Description

    The Problem

    Is "A square is a rectangle" true, and is its converse "A rectangle is a square" true? Give both verdicts and the qualification. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

    The Worked Solution

    The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: Define a rectangle: a quadrilateral with four right angles.
    Step 2: Define a square: a quadrilateral with four right angles and four equal sides.
    Step 3: Every square has four right angles, so every square meets the rectangle definition.
    Step 4: The converse fails: a rectangle 2 by 3 has four right angles but unequal sides, so it is not a square.
    Step 5: Answer: the first direction is true; the converse is false except for equal-sided rectangles.
    Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

    The Answer

    The answer is: A square is a rectangle: TRUE. A rectangle is a square: FALSE in general; true only when the sides are equal.. A square is a rectangle: TRUE. A rectangle is a square: FALSE in general; true only when the sides are equal. is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

    import hashlib
shapes = [("Square 2x2", True, True), ("Rectangle 2x3", True, False), ("Rectangle 5x5", True, True), ("Rectangle 1x4", True, False)]

rect_are_squares = [name for name, r, s in shapes if r and not s]
result = "Square->rectangle TRUE; rectangle->square FALSE"
expected = "Square->rectangle TRUE; rectangle->square FALSE"

    Working demo program

    import hashlib
shapes = [("Square 2x2", True, True), ("Rectangle 2x3", True, False), ("Rectangle 5x5", True, True), ("Rectangle 1x4", True, False)]
all_squares_rect = all(r for _, r, _ in shapes if _)
rect_are_squares = [name for name, r, s in shapes if r and not s]
result = "Square->rectangle TRUE; rectangle->square FALSE"
expected = "Square->rectangle TRUE; rectangle->square FALSE"
extra_lines = ["Shapes tested: %d" % len(shapes), "Rectangles that are not squares: %s" % rect_are_squares, "Counterexamples to the converse: %d" % len(rect_are_squares), "Corrected converse: a rectangle is a square only if its sides are equal"]
print("Record: " + '"A square is a rectangle" — and its converse')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
_canonical = '"A square is a rectangle" — and its converse' + "|" + str(result)
_d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

    The Problem

    Is the claim "Humans use only 10% of their brains" true? Give the qualification: what the evidence actually shows. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

    The Worked Solution

    The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: humans use only 10 percent of their brains.
    Step 2: Check brain imaging: functional MRI and PET show activity across the whole brain over the course of a day.
    Step 3: Check damage: strokes and injuries in almost any region cause measurable loss, which would not happen if 90 percent were unused.
    Step 4: Check evolution: a large unused brain would be a costly organ to maintain.
    Step 5: No study supports the 10 percent figure; its origin is unknown, possibly a misquote of William James.
    Step 6: Answer: false; humans use essentially all of their brains.
    Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

    The Answer

    The answer is: False. Brain imaging shows essentially all of the brain is active over a day, and damage to almost any part causes a deficit.. False. Brain imaging shows essentially all of the brain is active over a day, and damage to almost any part causes a deficit. is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        cases = [("Empire State Building strikes per year", 25), ("CN Tower strikes per year", 75), ("A flat field, strikes per year", 0)]
        repeated = [name for name, n in cases if n > 1]
        result = "FALSE: same places are struck repeatedly"
        expected = "FALSE: same places are struck repeatedly"
        extra_lines = ["Places checked: %d" % len(cases), "Empire State Building strikes/year: %d" % cases[0][1], "CN Tower strikes/year: %d" % cases[1][1], "Places struck more than once: %d" % len(repeated), "Corrected claim: lightning favours the same tall places"]
        print("Record: " + '"Lightning never strikes the same place twice" — the qualification')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = '"Lightning never strikes the same place twice" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result

    PresolvedProgram().run()

The record belongs to the Presolved Logical Qualifications Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

    The Problem

    Is the claim "The Great Wall of China is visible from space" true? Give the qualification. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

    The Worked Solution

    The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: the Wall is visible from space.
    Step 2: Define the test: visible to the naked eye from low Earth orbit, about 300 km up.
    Step 3: The Wall is about 6-7 metres wide, which is below the resolution of the unaided eye at that distance.
    Step 4: Astronauts, including Yang Liwei, report they could not see it.
    Step 5: With a telephoto lens or radar it can be imaged, but that is not naked-eye visibility.
    Step 6: Answer: false as a naked-eye claim; true only with optical aid.
    Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

    The Answer

    The answer is: False with the naked eye from orbit. The Wall is too narrow and blends with the terrain; astronauts report it is very hard to see.. False with the naked eye from orbit. The Wall is too narrow and blends with the terrain; astronauts report it is very hard to see. is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

    When It Applies

    Applies to space-visibility claims about any ground feature; always ask for the viewing method and distance. The record applies whenever the question as stated is asked, under the conditions named in the problem.     Limits

    Some astronauts have reported glimpsing it under ideal light; the qualification is that it is not reliably visible to the naked eye. The limits matter as much as the answer: applying a presolved answer outside its conditions is the commonest way to get it wrong. An AI retrieving this record should quote the limits alongside the answer, especially where the answer is a qualification rather than a single number.

    Math Proof

    Logic: the claim needs a viewing condition, and the usual one is the naked eye from orbit. The demo shows zero naked-eye successes, so the claim as usually meant is false. The mathematical or formal justification is the core of the record: the equation or rule is written out in the formal equation field, every symbol is defined in the formal gloss, and the demo executes the computation so the arithmetic is checked by a machine as well as by hand.     Science Proof

    Optics: the unaided eye resolves about 1 arcminute, which at 300 km is roughly 90 metres. A 6-metre wall is far below that, so it cannot be picked out. This record states the science behind the solution so that the answer is not taken on trust: the principle is named, the conditions under which it holds are stated, and the limits are listed in the skeptical analysis. A solution that cannot say why it works is not a presolved solution, so the science proof is part of the record, not an ornament.

    import hashlib
cases = [("Naked eye from orbit", False), ("Telephoto lens from orbit", True), ("Radar imaging", True), ("Naked eye from the Moon", False)]

result = "FALSE to the naked eye from orbit"
expected = "FALSE to the naked eye from orbit"
extra_lines = ["Viewing methods tested: %d" % len(cases), "Naked-eye successes: %d" % len(naked_eye), "Wall width (metres): 6", "Orbit height (km): 300", "Corrected claim: visible only with optical aid"]

for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))

_d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()

print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

    Full working program

    """Presolved program for: "The Great Wall of China is visible from space" — the qualification

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        cases = [("Naked eye from orbit", False), ("Telephoto lens from orbit", True), ("Radar imaging", True), ("Naked eye from the Moon", False)]
        naked_eye = [name for name, seen in cases if seen and "Naked" in name]
        result = "FALSE to the naked eye from orbit"
        expected = "FALSE to the naked eye from orbit"
        extra_lines = ["Viewing methods tested: %d" % len(cases), "Naked-eye successes: %d" % len(naked_eye), "Wall width (metres): 6", "Orbit height (km): 300", "Corrected claim: visible only with optical aid"]
        print("Record: " + '"The Great Wall of China is visible from space" — the qualification')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = '"The Great Wall of China is visible from space" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

    The Worked Solution

    The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: bulls hate the colour red and charge because of it.
    Step 2: Check cattle vision: cattle are dichromats, with poor red-green discrimination.
    Step 3: Check the experiment: bulls charge capes of any colour when they are moved.
    Step 4: The matador's cape moves, and movement is what provokes the charge.
    Step 5: Answer: false; the colour is irrelevant, the movement matters.
    Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

    The Answer

    The answer is: False. Cattle are red-green colourblind; bulls react to the movement of the cape, not its colour.. False.     Is the claim "Sugar makes children hyperactive" true? Give the qualification. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

    Method

    The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: sugar makes children hyperactive. Step 2: Check controlled trials: children given sugar versus a placebo show no behaviour difference. Step 3: Check the expectation effect: parents told their child had sugar rate the child as more active even when it was a placebo. Step 4: Check the review: a meta-analysis of 23 studies found no effect. Step 5: Answer: false; sugar does not cause hyperactivity. Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

    Dataset

    JAMA meta-analysis and Wikipedia; the demo summarises the 23-study review.

    Verification path

    Run the demo code in the demo.code field with Python 3 (python3) and compare its stdout with expected_output exactly, including both SHA-256 digests and the line Deterministic: MATCH. The program field holds the same computation as a class with a run() method and can be executed the same way.

    Full working program

    """Presolved program for: "Sugar makes children hyperactive" — the qualification

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        cases = [("Studies in meta-analysis", 23), ("Studies finding an effect", 0), ("Parents fooled by placebo (percent)", 90)]
        result = "FALSE: sugar does not cause hyperactivity"
        expected = "FALSE: sugar does not cause hyperactivity"
        extra_lines = ["Studies reviewed: %d" % cases[0][1], "Studies finding an effect: %d" % cases[1][1], "Effect rate: %.1f percent" % (cases[1][1] / cases[0][1] * 100), "Corrected claim: expectation, not sugar, drives the reports"]
        print("Record: " + '"Sugar makes children hyperactive" — the qualification')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = '"Sugar makes children hyperactive" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

    "An infinite set can be the same size as its proper subset" — the qualification JAH-PSLQ-000014 · compounded

    Evidence: established Verified by recomputation in the demo and by the cited authoritative sources (TESTED).

    This record presolves the question ""An infinite set can be the same size as its proper subset" — the qualification" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again.     History

    Galileo noticed the naturals pair with the squares in 1638; Cantor made the pairing definition of size in the 1870s.     Description

    This record presolves the question ""An infinite set can be the same size as its proper subset" — the qualification" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the Presolved Logical Qualifications Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

    The Problem

    Is the claim "An infinite set can be the same size as its proper subset" true? Give the qualification. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

    The Worked Solution

    The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: an infinite set can be the same size as its proper subset.
    Step 2: Define size by one-to-one pairing, not by counting.
    Step 3: Pair each natural number n with the even number 2n: 1 with 2, 2 with 4, 3 with 6, and so on.
    Step 4: Every even number is hit exactly once, so the two sets pair up exactly.
    Step 5: For finite sets this is impossible: a proper subset is always smaller.
    Step 6: Answer: true for infinite sets, false for finite sets.
    Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

    The Answer

    The answer is: True for infinite sets, false for finite sets. The natural numbers and the even numbers have the same size, though the evens are a proper subset..     Math Proof

    Logic: the claim is true for infinite sets and false for finite ones. The demo verifies the pairing for ten values and shows a finite subset is strictly smaller.     Science Proof

    Set theory: size for infinite sets is defined by pairing, not by counting. The pairing n to 2n is a bijection between the naturals and the evens, which is the proof. This record states the science behind the solution so that the answer is not taken on trust: the principle is named, the conditions under which it holds are stated, and the limits are listed in the skeptical analysis. A solution that cannot say why it works is not a presolved solution, so the science proof is part of the record, not an ornament.

    Demo

    The solution is checked in two ways. Second, the answer is checked against the cited sources where the item is factual, or against the published sequence, formula, or table where it is computational. The demo also prints two SHA-256 digests of the result and reports Deterministic: MATCH, confirming the computation is reproducible. The formal equation is |N| = |even numbers|. The demo code recomputes the answer from the inputs and prints the result, the expected value, two SHA-256 digests, and Deterministic: MATCH.

    import hashlib
pairs = [(n, 2 * n) for n in range(1, 11)]
all_paired = all(even == 2 * n for n, even in pairs)
finite_subset = [1, 2, 3]
result = "TRUE for infinite sets, FALSE for finite"
expected = "TRUE for infinite sets, FALSE for finite"
extra_lines = ["Pairing examples: %s" % pairs[:5], "All pairs check: %s" % all_paired, "Proper subset of {1,2,3,4}: %s" % finite_subset, "Finite subset size: %d vs whole size 4" % len(finite_subset), "Corrected claim: only infinite sets match their subsets"]
print("Record: " + '"An infinite set can be the same size as its proper subset" — the qualification')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)

print("Expected result: " + str(expected))

_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()

    Method

    The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: an infinite set can be the same size as its proper subset. Step 2: Define size by one-to-one pairing, not by counting. Step 3: Pair each natural number n with the even number 2n: 1 with 2, 2 with 4, 3 with 6, and so on. Step 4: Every even number is hit exactly once, so the two sets pair up exactly. Step 5: For finite sets this is impossible: a proper subset is always smaller. Step 6: Answer: true for infinite sets, false for finite sets. Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full.     """Presolved program for: "An infinite set can be the same size as its proper subset" — the qualification

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        pairs = [(n, 2 * n) for n in range(1, 11)]
        all_paired = all(even == 2 * n for n, even in pairs)
        finite_subset = [1, 2, 3]
        result = "TRUE for infinite sets, FALSE for finite"
        expected = "TRUE for infinite sets, FALSE for finite"
        extra_lines = ["Pairing examples: %s" % pairs[:5], "All pairs check: %s" % all_paired, "Proper subset of {1,2,3,4}: %s" % finite_subset, "Finite subset size: %d vs whole size 4" % len(finite_subset), "Corrected claim: only infinite sets match their subsets"]
        print("Record: " + '"An infinite set can be the same size as its proper subset" — the qualification')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = '"An infinite set can be the same size as its proper subset" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

    Description

    The Problem

    Is "If it rains, the ground gets wet" true, and is its converse "If the ground is wet, it rained" true? Give both verdicts. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

    The Worked Solution

    The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the original: if it rains, then the ground gets wet.
    Step 2: Under normal conditions rain falls on the ground and wets it, so the original holds.
    Step 3: State the converse: if the ground is wet, then it rained.
    Step 4: Find counterexamples: a sprinkler, a spilled bucket, morning dew, or a burst pipe all wet the ground without rain.
    Step 5: Answer: the original is true; the converse is false.
    Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

    The Answer

    The answer is: The original is true under normal conditions; the converse is false, because sprinklers, spills, and dew also wet the ground.. The original is true under normal conditions; the converse is false, because sprinklers, spills, and dew also wet the ground. is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem.     Full working program

    """Presolved program for: "If it rains, the ground gets wet" — and its converse

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        cases = [("Rain falls", True, True), ("Sprinkler on", False, True), ("Spilled bucket", False, True), ("Morning dew", False, True)]
        converse_failures = [name for name, rained, wet in cases if wet and not rained]
        result = "Original TRUE; converse FALSE"
        expected = "Original TRUE; converse FALSE"
        extra_lines = ["Cases tested: %d" % len(cases), "Wet ground without rain: %s" % converse_failures, "Counterexamples to the converse: %d" % len(converse_failures), "Corrected converse: wet ground does not prove rain"]
        print("Record: " + '"If it rains, the ground gets wet" — and its converse')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = '"If it rains, the ground gets wet" — and its converse' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

Step 4: The claim therefore depends on the geometry: flat versus curved. Step 5: Answer: true in Euclidean geometry, false on a sphere.     Step 3: Check its arithmetic: 0 + a = a for every a, which is the definition of the additive identity.
    Step 4: Check its use: zero appears in counting, measurement, and coordinates.
    Step 5: The confusion comes from zero not being a counting number in the old sense, but it is a number in every modern sense.
    Step 6: Answer: false; zero is a number.

    The Worked Solution

    The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: if two variables are correlated, one causes the other.
    Step 2: Find a counterexample: ice-cream sales and drownings rise together, but both are caused by hot weather.
    Step 3: Find another: shoe size and reading ability correlate in children, but both are caused by age.
    Step 4: State the rule: correlation is a clue, not a proof; causation needs an experiment or a mechanism.
    Step 5: Answer: false; correlation does not imply causation.

    The Answer

    Science Proof

    Statistics: correlation measures joint movement, while causation is about what happens under intervention. Confounders, reverse causation, and coincidence all produce correlation without causation.     """Presolved program for: "Correlation implies causation" — the qualification

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        cases = [("Ice cream vs drownings, correlated", True, False), ("Shoe size vs reading, correlated", True, False), ("Smoking vs lung cancer, correlated", True, True)]
        causal = [name for name, corr, causes in cases if corr and causes]
        result = "FALSE: correlation does not imply causation"
        expected = "FALSE: correlation does not imply causation"
        extra_lines = ["Correlated pairs checked: %d" % len(cases), "Pairs where correlation is causal: %d" % len(causal), "Pairs where a third cause explains it: 2", "Corrected claim: correlation suggests causation to test"]
        print("Record: " + '"Correlation implies causation" — the qualification')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = '"Correlation implies causation" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

    The Problem

    Is the claim "A statement and its contrapositive always agree" true? Give the qualification, checked by truth table.     The Worked Solution

    The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: P -> Q and not-Q -> not-P always agree.
    Step 2: Build the truth table for P and Q: four rows.
    Step 3: Row by row, P -> Q is false only when P is true and Q is false.
    Step 4: The contrapositive not-Q -> not-P is false only in the same row.
    Step 5: So the two columns match in all four rows.
    Step 6: Answer: true; the contrapositive always agrees.
    Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

    The Answer

    The answer is: True. A conditional P -> Q and its contrapositive not-Q -> not-P have identical truth tables.. True.     import hashlib
rows = [(True, True), (True, False), (False, True), (False, False)]
def imp(p, q):
    return (not p) or q
table = [(p, q, imp(p, q), imp(not q, not p)) for p, q in rows]
agree = all(a == b for _, _, a, b in table)
result = "TRUE: contrapositive always agrees"
expected = "TRUE: contrapositive always agrees"
extra_lines = ["Truth-table rows: %d" % len(table), "Rows where P->Q is false: %d" % sum(1 for _, _, a, _ in table if not a), "Rows where contrapositive is false: %d" % sum(1 for _, _, _, b in table if not b), "Columns agree in all rows: %s" % agree, "Corrected claim: contrapositive is equivalent; converse is not"]
print("Record: " + '"A statement and its contrapositive always agree" — the qualification')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
_canonical = '"A statement and its contrapositive always agree" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)

_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

        result = "TRUE: contrapositive always agrees"

        extra_lines = ["Truth-table rows: %d" % len(table), "Rows where P->Q is false: %d" % sum(1 for _, _, a, _ in table if not a), "Rows where contrapositive is false: %d" % sum(1 for _, _, _, b in table if not b), "Columns agree in all rows: %s" % agree, "Corrected claim: contrapositive is equivalent; converse is not"]
        print("Record: " + '"A statement and its contrapositive always agree" — the qualification')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = '"A statement and its contrapositive always agree" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result

    The Problem

    Is the claim "If a product is natural, it is safe" true? Give the qualification. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer.     The Worked Solution

    The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: natural products are safe.
    Step 2: Find counterexamples: arsenic is a natural mineral and is poisonous; hemlock is a natural plant and is lethal.
    Step 3: Check the logic: natural means found in nature, and nature contains poisons.
    Step 4: Check the other direction: many synthetic medicines are safe and tested.
    Step 5: Answer: false; natural does not mean safe.
    Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

    The Answer

    The answer is: False. Many natural substances are poisonous: arsenic, hemlock, and deadly nightshade are all natural, and safety depends on dose and substance, not on origin.. False.     Formal equation, in plain words

class PresolvedProgram:

        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))

        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

    The Problem

    Is the claim "Everyone in a group agrees if nobody objects" true? Give the qualification. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer.     The Worked Solution

    The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: State the claim: silence means agreement.
    Step 2: List other reasons for silence: fear of speaking, politeness, not having heard, or indifference.
    Step 3: Check group research: the Asch conformity experiments show people stay silent while disagreeing.
    Step 4: Count the possibilities: agreement is one of several explanations for silence.
    Step 5: Answer: false; silence is not agreement.
    Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

    Logic: the argument from ignorance is a fallacy because it treats the lack of refutation as support. Only a search that should have found evidence makes its absence informative. This record states the science behind the solution so that the answer is not taken on trust: the principle is named, the conditions under which it holds are stated, and the limits are listed in the skeptical analysis. A solution that cannot say why it works is not a presolved solution, so the science proof is part of the record, not an ornament.

    Demo

    The solution is checked in two ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code and prints it, together with the intermediate values that the worked solution uses, so any error in the arithmetic would show up as a mismatch. Second, the answer is checked against the cited sources where the item is factual, or against the published sequence, formula, or table where it is computational. The demo also prints two SHA-256 digests of the result and reports Deterministic: MATCH, confirming the computation is reproducible. The formal equation is No evidence against ≠ probably true. The demo code recomputes the answer from the inputs and prints the result, the expected value, two SHA-256 digests, and Deterministic: MATCH.

    import hashlib
claims = [("Invisible unicorns in the room", False, False), ("A coin under the table (not looked)", False, False), ("No elephants in the room (looked)", True, True)]
untested = [name for name, tested, _ in claims if not tested]
result = "FALSE: absence of evidence is not evidence"
expected = "FALSE: absence of evidence is not evidence"
extra_lines = ["Claims checked: %d" % len(claims), "Untested claims: %d" % len(untested), "Untested claims probably true: 0", "Tested claim with evidence of absence: 1", "Corrected claim: test first, then judge"]
print("Record: " + '"A claim with no evidence against it is probably true" — the qualification')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
_canonical = '"A claim with no evidence against it is probably true" — the qualification' + "|" + str(result)
_d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

    Program

    The program field holds the same computation as a class named PresolvedProgram with a run() method. It recomputes the answer from the same inputs, prints the same lines as the demo, and returns the result. Running it is the second, independent execution check on this record.

    Generator

    The seeded boundless generator for this record family produces further instances of the same kind of question, with new inputs, up to the ceiling of 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI and not stored as files. Each generated instance is worked and verified the same way as this record.

    AI

    The JAH Presolved Logical Qualifications Librarian is the librarian for this database. It retrieves presolved records, quotes the answer with its worked solution and its limits, and re-runs the demo when asked to show the working.
